Answer: (d) \(B'\). Y = 1 in rows 000, 001, 100, 101: exactly the rows with B = 0, whatever A and C are. \(Y=B'\).

Skill 4 Simplify to the fewest literals

Example 2.1

Simplifying means applying the theorems until no rule makes the expression shorter. A handful of patterns does almost all the work.

Method
  1. Multiply out, then look for \(x+x'=1\) and \(xx'=0\).
  2. Absorption: \(x+xy=x\) and \(x(x+y)=x\).
  3. \(x+x'y=x+y\) and \(x(x'+y)=xy\).
  4. Consensus: \(xy+x'z+yz=xy+x'z\). The term made of the two partners of \(x\) and \(x'\) can be dropped.
  5. Factor out a common variable and use \(1+y=1\).
  6. If unsure, check two or three rows of the truth table.
Worked example · read it, then cover it and redo it on paper
Example 2.1Lec 3, slide 33

Simplify the five expressions to a minimum number of literals: \(x(x'+y)\); \(x+x'y\); \((x+y)(x+y')\); \(xy+x'z+yz\); \((x+y)(x'+z)(y+z)\).

Find: the simplified forms

Answer: \(xy\); \(x+y\); \(x\); \(xy+x'z\); \((x+y)(x'+z)\)

Solution on paper

1.

\[\begin{aligned}x(x'+y) &= xx'+xy && \text{distributive} \\[3pt] &= 0+xy && \text{\(xx'=0\)} \\[3pt] &= \boxed{xy}\end{aligned}\]

2.

\[\begin{aligned}x+x'y &= (x+x')(x+y) && \text{\(a+bc=(a+b)(a+c)\)} \\[3pt] &= 1\cdot(x+y) && \text{\(x+x'=1\)} \\[3pt] &= \boxed{x+y}\end{aligned}\]

3.

\[\begin{aligned}(x+y)(x+y') &= x+xy+xy'+yy' && \text{multiply out} \\[3pt] &= x(1+y+y') && \text{\(yy'=0\), factor x} \\[3pt] &= \boxed{x}\end{aligned}\]

4.

\[\begin{aligned}xy+x'z+yz &= xy+x'z+yz(x+x') \\[3pt] &= xy+x'z+xyz+x'yz \\[3pt] &= xy(1+z)+x'z(1+y) \\[3pt] &= \boxed{xy+x'z}\end{aligned}\]

5.

\[\begin{aligned}(x+y)(x'+z)(y+z) &= \boxed{(x+y)(x'+z)} && \text{by duality from number 4 (consensus theorem)}\end{aligned}\]
The method, step by step: (1) Multiplying out gives \(xx'+xy\), and \(xx'=0\) (step 1). (2) \(a+bc=(a+b)(a+c)\) gives \((x+x')(x+y)=x+y\) (step 3). (3) Multiplying out and factoring gives \(x(1+y+y')=x\) (step 5). (4) is the consensus pattern (step 4), and (5) is its dual.
From the past midterms · same skill, exam format
2023 Q31Which of the following is the simplified form of the Boolean expression \((A+B'+A'B)\cdot C\)?
  • (a) \(C'\)
  • (b) \(C\)
  • (c) 0
  • (d) 1
  • (e) None
Answer: (b) \(C\). \(A+B'+A'B=A+B'+B=1\) (since \(B'+A'B=B'+A'\) and \(A+A'=1\)). So the expression is \(1\cdot C=C\).
2023 Q32Simplified value of \(Y=AB'+(A'+B)C\) is
  • (a) \(AB'+C\)
  • (b) \(AB+AC\)
  • (c) \(A'B+AC'\)
  • (d) \(AB+A\)
  • (e) None
Answer: (a) \(AB'+C\). \(A'+B=(AB')'\). So \(Y=AB'+(AB')'C=AB'+C\) by \(a+a'c=a+c\).
2021 Q18Simplified form of the function \(f=(x+y+xy)(x+z)\)
  • (a) \(x+yz\)
  • (b) \(x+y\)
  • (c) \(y+xz\)
  • (d) \(x+xyz\)
Answer: (a) \(x+yz\). \(x+y+xy=x+y\) (absorption), so \(f=(x+y)(x+z)=x+yz\) by the distributive law.
2020 Q21\((A+B)(A'\cdot B')=\,?\)
  • (a) 0
  • (b) 1
  • (c) \(AB\)
  • (d) \(AB'\)
Answer: (a) 0. \(A'B'=(A+B)'\), and anything ANDed with its own complement is 0. Or multiply: \(AA'B'+BA'B'=0+0\).

Skill 5 Complement of a function

Examples 2.2 and 2.3

The complement \(F'\) is 1 exactly where \(F\) is 0. DeMorgan's theorem builds it: break the bar and change the sign.

Method
  1. DeMorgan: \((x+y)'=x'y'\) and \((xy)'=x'+y'\), for any number of terms.
  2. Work from the outside in: first the main + or ·, then each bracket.
  3. Short way (Example 2.3): take the dual of \(F\) (swap + ↔ ·, keep the brackets), then prime every literal (\(x \leftrightarrow x'\)).
  4. Check: \(F+F'\) must be 1 in every row.
Worked example · read it, then cover it and redo it on paper
Example 2.2Lec 3, slide 35

Find the complement of \(F_1=x'yz'+x'y'z\) and of \(F_2=x(y'z'+yz)\) with DeMorgan's theorem.

Find: \(F_1'\) and \(F_2'\)

Answer: \(F_1'=(x+y'+z)(x+y+z')\); \(F_2'=x'+yz'+y'z\)

Solution on paper
\[\begin{aligned}F_1' &= (x'yz'+x'y'z)' \\[3pt] &= (x'yz')'\,(x'y'z)' && \text{DeMorgan on the +} \\[3pt] &= \boxed{(x+y'+z)(x+y+z')} && \text{DeMorgan on each product}\end{aligned}\]
\[\begin{aligned}F_2' &= [x(y'z'+yz)]' \\[3pt] &= x'+(y'z'+yz)' && \text{DeMorgan on the product} \\[3pt] &= x'+(y'z')'(yz)' \\[3pt] &= x'+(y+z)(y'+z') \\[3pt] &= \boxed{x'+yz'+y'z} && \text{multiply out, \(yy'=zz'=0\)}\end{aligned}\]
The method, step by step: For \(F_1'\) the bar over the + turns into a product of two barred terms (step 2). Each barred product becomes a sum with every literal flipped (step 1): \((x+y'+z)(x+y+z')\).
Your turn · solve on paper first, then open the answer
Example 2.3Lec 3, slide 35

Find the same complements by the simpler procedure: take the dual, then complement each literal.

Find: \(F_1'\) and \(F_2'\)

Hint: the dual of \(F_1\) is \((x'+y+z')(x'+y'+z)\). Now prime every literal, and do the same for \(F_2\).

Answer: the same results as Example 2.2

Solution on paper
\(F_1=x'yz'+x'y'z\)\(F_2=x(y'z'+yz)\)
Dual\((x'+y+z')(x'+y'+z)\)\(x+(y'+z')(y+z)\)
Complement each literal\((x+y'+z)(x+y+z')=F_1'\)\(x'+(y+z)(y'+z')=F_2'\)
From the past midterms · same skill, exam format
2021 Q8Complement of the expression \(A'B+CD'\) is
  • (a) \((AB')(C'D)\)
  • (b) \((A+B')(C+D')\)
  • (c) \((A'+B)(C+D')\)
  • (d) \((A+B')(C'+D)\)
Answer: (d) \((A+B')(C'+D)\). Dual, then complement every literal: \((A'B+CD')'=(A+B')(C'+D)\).
2023 Q40Simplify the expression \(\overline{\overline{AB}+C}\) using DeMorgan's theorems.
  • (a) \((A+B)C(A+B)C\)
  • (b) \(AB+\overline C\)
  • (c) \(\overline{AB}+C\)
  • (d) \(AB\overline C\)
  • (e) None
Answer: (d) \(AB\overline C\). \(\overline{\overline{AB}+C}=\overline{\overline{AB}}\cdot\overline C=AB\overline C\).
2020 Q8Which of the following is based on DeMorgan's theorems?
  • (a) \(\overline{X+Y}=\overline X+\overline Y\)
  • (b) \(X+0=X\)
  • (c) \(\overline{XY}=\overline X+\overline Y\)
  • (d) \(X(1)=X\)
Answer: (c) \(\overline{XY}=\overline X+\overline Y\). DeMorgan: break the bar and change the sign. \(\overline{XY}=\overline X+\overline Y\) and \(\overline{X+Y}=\overline X\,\overline Y\). Option (a) forgets to change the + into ·.
2021 Q24Which step in this reduction process is using DeMorgan's theorem? \(X=\overline{AB}\;\overline{(B+C)}\); Step 1: \(X=(\overline A+\overline B)(\overline B\,\overline C)\); Step 2: \(X=\overline A\,\overline B\,\overline C+\overline B\,\overline B\,\overline C\); Step 3: \(X=\overline B\,\overline C(\overline A+1)\); Step 4: \(X=\overline B\,\overline C\)
  • (a) Step 2
  • (b) Step 4
  • (c) Step 3
  • (d) Step 1
Answer: (d) Step 1. Step 1 turns \(\overline{AB}\) into \((\overline A+\overline B)\) and \(\overline{B+C}\) into \((\overline B\,\overline C)\): that is DeMorgan. Step 2 is the distributive law, step 3 is factoring, step 4 uses \(\overline A+1=1\).

Formula sheet for revision

Everything above in one place, with the doctor's steps and the traps. Revise from it once you can do the skills; it is hard to learn from it the first time.

Postulates and theorems to memorise (Table 2.1)
  • Postulate 2 (identity): \(x+0=x\)  |  \(x\cdot1=x\)
  • Postulate 5 (complement): \(x+x'=1\)  |  \(x\cdot x'=0\)
  • Theorem 1 (idempotent): \(x+x=x\)  |  \(x\cdot x=x\)
  • Theorem 2 (annulment): \(x+1=1\)  |  \(x\cdot0=0\)
  • Theorem 3 (involution): \((x')'=x\)
  • Postulate 3 (commutative): \(x+y=y+x\)  |  \(xy=yx\)
  • Theorem 4 (associative): \(x+(y+z)=(x+y)+z\)  |  \(x(yz)=(xy)z\)
  • Postulate 4 (distributive): \(x(y+z)=xy+xz\)  |  \(x+yz=(x+y)(x+z)\)
  • Theorem 5 (DeMorgan): \((x+y)'=x'y'\)  |  \((xy)'=x'+y'\)
  • Theorem 6 (absorption): \(x+xy=x\)  |  \(x(x+y)=x\)
  • Used in Example 2.1: \(x+x'y=x+y\)  |  \(x(x'+y)=xy\)
  • Consensus: \(xy+x'z+yz=xy+x'z\)  |  \((x+y)(x'+z)(y+z)=(x+y)(x'+z)\)
  • Operator precedence: parentheses, then NOT, then AND, then OR.
  • Dual: swap + ↔ · and 0 ↔ 1, keep the parentheses where they are.
  • Complement of a function: take the dual and complement every literal (generalised DeMorgan): \((A+B+C+\dots)'=A'B'C'\dots\), \((ABC\dots)'=A'+B'+C'+\dots\)
The doctor's steps for a proof
  1. For a proof “by postulates”: change one side step by step, and write the number of the postulate or theorem used at the end of every line (2(b), 5(a), 4(b) …), exactly as on slides 12 to 19.
  2. For a proof “by truth table”: list all input rows, add one column for each part, and show that the two final columns are identical.
  3. To multiply a term by 1 use \(x+x'=1\); to add 0 use \(xx'=0\). These two tricks start most proofs.
Traps
  • \((xy)'\) is not \(x'y'\). Break the bar and change the sign: \((xy)'=x'+y'\).
  • The dual is not the complement. The dual swaps operators only; the complement also primes every literal.
  • \(x+yz=(x+y)(x+z)\) is true in Boolean algebra although it is false in ordinary algebra.
  • When forming a dual, do not move the parentheses: the dual of \(a+(bc)\) is \(a(b+c)\).
← Lecture 2 · Codes and gatesLecture 4 · Canonical forms →

Lecture 4 · Canonical and standard forms

Source: “ELE121_Lec 4.pdf” (26 slides). Chapter 2, section 2.6: minterms, maxterms, sum of minterms, product of maxterms, conversion, standard forms.

In plain words

Every row of a truth table has a number (its inputs read as a binary number). A minterm is the AND term that is 1 in that one row only. A maxterm is the OR term that is 0 in that one row only.

Any function can be written in two exact ways: OR together the minterms of the rows where the function is 1 (sum of minterms, Σ), or AND together the maxterms of the rows where the function is 0 (product of maxterms, Π). These two are called canonical forms. A standard form (SOP or POS) is the same idea but the terms are allowed to miss some variables, so it is shorter.

How to study this lecture

The lecture is 5 skills, in order. For each skill: read the idea and the method, follow the worked example, then solve the Your turn questions on paper before you open the answer. Tick the box at the end of a skill when you can solve its questions without help.

  1. Name minterms and maxterms
  2. Truth table → Σ and Π
  3. Expression → sum of minterms
  4. Expression → product of maxterms
  5. Standard forms and two-level circuits

Exam weight: minterms, maxterms and SOP / POS forms: 15 of the 143 multiple-choice questions in the four past midterms. The written function question of 2020 and 2025 asks for the sum of minterms.

Already know it? Jump to the formula sheet, or to revise.

Skill 1 Name minterms and maxterms

Slides 3 to 8

With \(n\) variables there are \(2^{\,n}\) input rows. Row \(j\) has one minterm \(m_j\), a product that is 1 only in that row, and one maxterm \(M_j\), a sum that is 0 only in that row.

Method
  1. Write \(j\) in binary with \(n\) bits, in the standard variable order (the first variable is the MSB).
  2. Minterm: AND all the variables, and prime those whose bit is 0.
  3. Maxterm: OR all the variables, and prime those whose bit is 1.
  4. Backwards: in a minterm, unprimed = 1 and primed = 0; in a maxterm, unprimed = 0 and primed = 1. Turn the bits into a decimal number.
  5. \(M_j=m_j'\): each maxterm is the complement of the minterm with the same number.

Table 2.3: minterms and maxterms for three variables.

xyzMintermNameMaxtermName
000\(x'y'z'\)\(m_0\)\(x+y+z\)\(M_0\)
001\(x'y'z\)\(m_1\)\(x+y+z'\)\(M_1\)
010\(x'yz'\)\(m_2\)\(x+y'+z\)\(M_2\)
011\(x'yz\)\(m_3\)\(x+y'+z'\)\(M_3\)
100\(xy'z'\)\(m_4\)\(x'+y+z\)\(M_4\)
101\(xy'z\)\(m_5\)\(x'+y+z'\)\(M_5\)
110\(xyz'\)\(m_6\)\(x'+y'+z\)\(M_6\)
111\(xyz\)\(m_7\)\(x'+y'+z'\)\(M_7\)
Worked example · read it, then cover it and redo it on paper
Slide 7Lec 4

Standard order X, Y, Z. Fill in: \(m_2=m_{010}=?\)   \(M_3=M_{011}=?\)   \(XY\overline Z=m_?\)   \(X+Y+\overline Z=M_?\)

Find: the four answers

Answer: \(m_2=\overline XY\overline Z\); \(M_3=X+\overline Y+\overline Z\); \(XY\overline Z=m_6\); \(X+Y+\overline Z=M_1\)

Solution on paper
ItemBinary patternRuleResult
\(m_2\)010minterm: prime where the bit is 0\(X'YZ'\)
\(M_3\)011maxterm: prime where the bit is 1\(X+Y'+Z'\)
\(XYZ'\)110unprimed = 1, primed = 0\(m_6\)
\(X+Y+Z'\)001maxterm: unprimed = 0, primed = 1\(M_1\)
The method, step by step: \(m_2\): 2 = 010, prime where the bit is 0, giving \(X'YZ'\) (step 2). \(M_3\): 3 = 011, prime where the bit is 1, giving \(X+Y'+Z'\) (step 3). \(XYZ'\) reads 110 = 6 and \(X+Y+Z'\) reads 001 = 1 (step 4).

Skill 2 Truth table → Σ and Π

Slides 5 and 6

Read the truth table: the rows where \(F=1\) are its minterms, and the rows where \(F=0\) are its maxterms.

Method
  1. \(F=\Sigma\)(rows where \(F=1\)).
  2. \(F=\Pi\)(rows where \(F=0\)).
  3. Σ ↔ Π: swap the symbol and list the missing numbers.
  4. Complement \(F'\): keep the symbol and list the missing numbers.
Worked example · read it, then cover it and redo it on paper
Slides 5 and 6 (Table 2.4)Lec 4

For \(f_1=x'y'z+xy'z'+xyz\) and \(f_2=x'yz+xy'z+xyz'+xyz\): give the truth table, the sum of minterms, the complement, and the product of maxterms.

Find: Σ and Π forms of \(f_1\) and \(f_2\)

Answer: \(f_1=\Sigma(1, 4, 7)=\Pi(0, 2, 3, 5, 6)\); \(f_2=\Sigma(3, 5, 6, 7)=\Pi(0, 1, 2, 4)\)

Solution on paper
\(x\)\(y\)\(z\)\(f_1\)\(f_2\)
00000
00110
01000
01101
10010
10101
11001
11111
\[\begin{aligned}f_1 &= m_1+m_4+m_7\end{aligned}\]
\[\begin{aligned}f_1' &= m_0+m_2+m_3+m_5+m_6 \\[3pt] &= x'y'z'+x'yz'+x'yz+xy'z+xyz'\end{aligned}\]
\[\begin{aligned}f_1 &= (f_1')' \\[3pt] &= (x+y+z)(x+y'+z)(x+y'+z')(x'+y+z')(x'+y'+z) && \text{DeMorgan} \\[3pt] &= \boxed{M_0M_2M_3M_5M_6}\end{aligned}\]
\[\begin{aligned}f_2 &= m_3+m_5+m_6+m_7 \\[3pt] &= (x+y+z)(x+y+z')(x+y'+z)(x'+y+z) \\[3pt] &= \boxed{M_0M_1M_2M_4}\end{aligned}\]
The method, step by step: \(f_1\) is 1 in rows 1, 4 and 7, so \(f_1=\Sigma(1,4,7)\) (step 1). The other rows 0, 2, 3, 5, 6 give \(\Pi(0,2,3,5,6)\) (step 2).
From the past midterms · same skill, exam format
2023 Q6For the minterm designation \(Y=\Sigma m(1,3,5,7)\) the complete expression is
  • (a) \(Y=A'B'C+AB'C\)
  • (b) \(Y=A'B'C+AB'C+ABC+A'BC\)
  • (c) \(Y=A'B'C'+A'B'C+A'BC+AB'C\)
  • (d) \(Y=A'B'C'+ABC+A'B'C+AB'\)
  • (e) None
Answer: (b) \(Y=A'B'C+AB'C+ABC+A'BC\). 001, 011, 101, 111 → \(A'B'C+A'BC+AB'C+ABC\). The function simplifies to \(Y=C\).
2023 Q20A switching function \(f(A,B,C,D)=\) \((A'+B'+C+D)\) \((A'+B+C'+D)\) \((A'+B+C'+D)\) \((A+B'+C+D)\) \((A'+B'+C'+D)\)
  • (a) \(\Sigma(1,3,5,11)\)
  • (b) \(\Pi(1,3,5,11)\)
  • (c) \(\Sigma(4,10,12,14)\)
  • (d) \(\Pi(4,10,12,14)\)
  • (e) None
Answer: (d) \(\Pi(4,10,12,14)\). A maxterm is primed where the bit is 1: \(A'+B'+C+D\) → 1100 = 12; \(A'+B+C'+D\) → 1010 = 10 (it appears twice); \(A+B'+C+D\) → 0100 = 4; \(A'+B'+C'+D\) → 1110 = 14. \(f=\Pi(4,10,12,14)\).
2021 Q31A switching function \(f(A,B,C,D)=\) \(A'B'CD+A'BC'D\) \(+\,A'BC'D+AB'CD\) \(+\,A'B'C'D\)
  • (a) \(\Sigma(1,3,5,7,9)\)
  • (b) \(\Sigma(1,3,5,11)\)
  • (c) \(\Sigma(3,5,9,11)\)
  • (d) \(\Sigma(3,5,7,9,11)\)
Answer: (b) \(\Sigma(1,3,5,11)\). 0011 = 3, 0101 = 5 (written twice), 1011 = 11, 0001 = 1 → \(\Sigma(1,3,5,11)\).

Skill 3 Expression → sum of minterms

Example 2.4 and slide 11

A product term that misses a variable stands for several minterms. Expanding until every term holds all the variables gives the canonical sum of minterms.

Method
  1. For each product term, find the variables it misses.
  2. AND the term with \((v+v')\) for each missing \(v\), and multiply out.
  3. Delete repeated terms.
  4. Replace each full term by its number (Skill 1) and write \(F=\Sigma(\dots)\) in increasing order.

Faster: build the truth table and read the rows where \(F=1\) (Skill 2).

Worked example · read it, then cover it and redo it on paper
Example 2.4Lec 4, slides 9 and 15

Express \(F=A+B'C\) as a sum of minterms.

VariablesA, B, C

Find: \(F=\Sigma(\dots)\)

Answer: \(F(A,B,C)=\Sigma(1, 4, 5, 6, 7)\)

Solution on paper
\[\begin{aligned}F &= A(B+B')+B'C && \text{A misses B} \\[3pt] &= AB+AB'+B'C \\[3pt] &= AB(C+C')+AB'(C+C')+(A+A')B'C && \text{add the missing variable to each term} \\[3pt] &= ABC+ABC'+AB'C+AB'C'+AB'C+A'B'C \\[3pt] &= A'B'C+AB'C'+AB'C+ABC'+ABC && \text{delete the repeated \(AB'C\), put in order} \\[3pt] &= m_1+m_4+m_5+m_6+m_7 \\[3pt] &= \boxed{\Sigma(1,4,5,6,7)}\end{aligned}\]

Or build the truth table first (Table 2.5) and read the rows where F = 1:

\(A\)\(B\)\(C\)F
0000
0011
0100
0110
1001
1011
1101
1111
The method, step by step: \(A\) misses \(B\) and \(C\), so it becomes four terms; \(B'C\) misses \(A\) and becomes two (steps 1 and 2). \(AB'C\) appears twice and is kept once (step 3). The five terms are rows 1, 4, 5, 6, 7 (step 4).

  • (a) 2
  • (b) 3
  • (c) 4
  • (d) 5
  • (e) None
Answer: (c) 4. \(A+ABC=A\) (absorption). A alone covers the rows 100, 101, 110, 111: 4 minterms.
2021 Q12In the expression \(A+BC\), the total number of minterms will be
  • (a) 2
  • (b) 3
  • (c) 4
  • (d) 5
Answer: (d) 5. A gives rows 4, 5, 6, 7 and BC adds row 3: \(\Sigma(3,4,5,6,7)\), five minterms.

Skill 4 Expression → product of maxterms

Example 2.5 and slides 13 to 20

The same idea in dual form: turn the expression into a product of sums, then add the missing variables to each sum.

Method
  1. Use \(x+yz=(x+y)(x+z)\) to turn the expression into a product of sums.
  2. OR each sum that misses \(v\) with \(vv'\), and split it again with the same rule.
  3. Delete repeated sums.
  4. Replace each sum by its maxterm number (unprimed = 0, primed = 1) and write \(F=\Pi(\dots)\).

Shortcut: the maxterms are the rows where \(F=0\), that is, the numbers missing from the Σ list.

Worked example · read it, then cover it and redo it on paper
Example 2.5Lec 4, slides 12 and 21

Express \(F=xy+x'z\) as a product of maxterms.

Find: \(F=\Pi(\dots)\)

Answer: \(F(x,y,z)=\Pi(0, 2, 4, 5)\); also \(F=\Sigma(1, 3, 6, 7)\)

Solution on paper
\[\begin{aligned}F &= (xy+x')(xy+z) && \text{\(a+bc=(a+b)(a+c)\)} \\[3pt] &= (x+x')(y+x')(x+z)(y+z) && \text{again on each bracket} \\[3pt] &= (x'+y)(x+z)(y+z) && \text{\(x+x'=1\)}\end{aligned}\]

Each sum misses one variable. Add it as \(vv'\):

\[\begin{aligned}x'+y &= x'+y+zz'=(x'+y+z)(x'+y+z')\end{aligned}\]
\[\begin{aligned}x+z &= x+z+yy'=(x+y+z)(x+y'+z)\end{aligned}\]
\[\begin{aligned}y+z &= y+z+xx'=(x+y+z)(x'+y+z)\end{aligned}\]
\[\begin{aligned}F &= (x+y+z)(x+y'+z)(x'+y+z)(x'+y+z') && \text{delete repeated sums} \\[3pt] &= M_0M_2M_4M_5 \\[3pt] &= \boxed{\Pi(0,2,4,5)}\end{aligned}\]
\(x\)\(y\)\(z\)F
0000
0011
0100
0111
1000
1010
1101
1111

The zeros of the table are rows 0, 2, 4, 5, which confirms Π(0, 2, 4, 5). The slide's “Π(0, 2, 4, 6)” is a misprint.

The method, step by step: Applying \(x+yz=(x+y)(x+z)\) twice gives \((x'+y)(x+z)(y+z)\) (step 1). Each sum misses one variable and splits in two (step 2). The repeated \((x+y+z)\) is kept once (step 3), leaving \(M_0M_2M_4M_5\) (step 4).

Answer: (b) \((A+B+C)(A+\overline B+C)(A+\overline B+\overline C)\). The minterms are 7, 4, 5, 6, 1 → \(\Sigma(1,4,5,6,7)\). Missing: 0, 2, 3 → \(\Pi(0,2,3)=(A+B+C)(A+\overline B+C)(A+\overline B+\overline C)\).

Skill 5 Standard forms and two-level circuits

Slides 21 to 25

Canonical forms are exact but long. A standard form (a sum of products or a product of sums) may use shorter terms, and each one maps straight onto a two-level circuit.

Method
  1. Sum of products (SOP): products ORed together. Circuit: AND gates, then one OR gate.
  2. Product of sums (POS): sums ANDed together. Circuit: OR gates, then one AND gate.
  3. To shorten a canonical form, combine terms that differ in one variable only: \(xy+xy'=x\).
  4. Compare the cost in literals and gates.
Worked example · read it, then cover it and redo it on paper
Slide 23Lec 4

Simplify \(F(A,B,C)=\Sigma m(1,4,5,6,7)\) written as minterms into a standard SOP, and compare the cost.

Find: the simplified SOP

Answer: \(F=A+\overline BC\) (3 literals instead of 15)

Solution on paper
\[\begin{aligned}F &= \overline A\,\overline BC+A\overline B\,\overline C+A\overline BC+AB\overline C+ABC && \text{15 literals} \\[3pt] &= \overline A\,\overline BC+A\overline B(\overline C+C)+AB(\overline C+C) \\[3pt] &= \overline A\,\overline BC+A\overline B+AB \\[3pt] &= \overline A\,\overline BC+A && \text{\(A\overline B+AB=A\)} \\[3pt] &= (A+\overline A)(A+\overline BC) && \text{\(a+bc=(a+b)(a+c)\)} \\[3pt] &= \boxed{A+\overline BC} && \text{3 literals}\end{aligned}\]
The method, step by step: \(A\overline B\,\overline C+A\overline BC\) collapses to \(A\overline B\), and \(AB\overline C+ABC\) to \(AB\) (step 3). Then \(A\overline B+AB=A\) and \(A+\overline A\,\overline BC=A+\overline BC\). 15 literals become 3: one AND gate and one OR gate (step 4).
Your turn · solve on paper first, then open the answer

  • (b) 2, 3

2020 Q16The expression \(Y=(A+B)(B+C)(C+A)\) shows the _________ operation.
  • (a) AND
  • (b) POS

Formula sheet for revision

Everything above in one place, with the doctor's steps and the traps. Revise from it once you can do the skills; it is hard to learn from it the first time.

Rules to memorise
  • \(n\) variables → \(2^{\,n}\) minterms and \(2^{\,n}\) maxterms; \(2^{2^{\,n}}\) different functions.
  • Minterm \(m_j\): write the row number \(j\) in binary; a variable is primed where the bit is 0. Example \(m_5=m_{101}=xy'z\).
  • Maxterm \(M_j\): a variable is primed where the bit is 1. Example \(M_5=M_{101}=x'+y+z'\).
  • \(M_j=m_j'\) (each maxterm is the complement of the minterm with the same number).
  • \(F=\Sigma(\text{rows where }F=1)\) and \(F=\Pi(\text{rows where }F=0)\).
  • Conversion: swap Σ ↔ Π and list the missing numbers. \(F=\Sigma(1,4,5,6,7)=\Pi(0,2,3)\).
  • Complement: keep the symbol and list the missing numbers, or keep the numbers and swap the symbol. \(F'=\Sigma(0,2,3)=\Pi(1,4,5,6,7)\).
  • To expand a term that misses a variable \(v\): for a product, AND it with \((v+v')\); for a sum, OR it with \(vv'\) and use \(a+bc=(a+b)(a+c)\).
  • Standard order of the variables: alphabetical, first variable = most significant bit.
  • Two-level implementation: SOP → AND gates then one OR gate; POS → OR gates then one AND gate.
The doctor's steps: expression → sum of minterms
  1. Check every product term. If a variable is missing, multiply the term by (variable + its complement).
  2. Multiply out and delete repeated terms.
  3. Replace each full term by its number and write \(F=\Sigma(\dots)\) in increasing order.
  4. Faster alternative shown on slides 9, 15 and 16: build the truth table first and read the rows.
Traps
  • Priming the wrong way round. Minterm: 0 → prime. Maxterm: 1 → prime.
  • Slide 9 states Example 2.4 as “\(F=A+BC'\)” but solves \(F=A+B'C\). The function in the textbook and in the working is \(A+B'C\).
  • Slide 21 prints \(F(x,y,z)=\Pi(0,2,4,6)\) for \(F=xy+x'z\). The truth table beside it (and slide 12) gives \(\Pi(0,2,4,5)\).
  • The text layer of the same slide calls the function “\(xy+xz\)”; the function meant is \(xy+x'z\).
  • A term is in standard order only if its variables appear in the fixed order; \((b+a+\overline c)\) and \(acb\) are not.
← Lecture 3 · Boolean algebraLecture 5 · Other gates →

Lecture 5 · Other logic operations and gates

Source: “ELE121_Lec 5.pdf” (35 slides). Chapter 2, sections 2.7 and 2.8: the 16 functions of two variables, NAND and NOR, XOR and XNOR, multiple inputs, positive and negative logic.

In plain words

With two inputs there are only 16 possible truth tables, so only 16 possible gates. Eight of them are useful enough to be sold as standard gates. Two of these, NAND and NOR, are called universal: any circuit at all can be built from NAND gates only, or from NOR gates only.

XOR answers “are the two inputs different?” and XNOR answers “are they equal?”. With more than two inputs XOR becomes the odd function: it is 1 when an odd number of inputs are 1. That is exactly what a parity circuit needs.

How to study this lecture

The lecture is 5 skills, in order. For each skill: read the idea and the method, follow the worked example, then solve the Your turn questions on paper before you open the answer. Tick the box at the end of a skill when you can solve its questions without help.

  1. The 16 functions of two variables
  2. Why NAND and NOR are not associative
  3. Build any gate from NAND or NOR only
  4. XOR, XNOR, odd and even functions
  5. Positive and negative logic

Exam weight: NAND / NOR universality, gate counts and positive / negative logic: 15 of the 143 multiple-choice questions in the four past midterms, and the written questions of 2020 and 2025 end with a NAND circuit.

Already know it? Jump to the formula sheet, or to revise.

Skill 1 The 16 functions of two variables

Slides 3 to 7

Two inputs give 4 rows, and each row's output can be 0 or 1, so there are \(2^4=16\) possible functions. Eight of them are the standard gates.

Method
  1. \(n\) inputs → \(2^{\,n}\) rows → \(2^{2^{\,n}}\) different functions.
  2. Of the 16: two constants (0 and 1); transfer and complement, which each appear twice; inhibition and implication, which are not commutative or associative and so are not used as gates; and the standard gates.
  3. The eight standard gates: inverter (complement), buffer (transfer), AND, OR, NAND, NOR, XOR and XNOR (equivalence).

Tables 2.7 and 2.8: the 16 functions of two variables. The last column is the output for xy = 00, 01, 10, 11.

FunctionExpressionNameOutput column
\(F_{0}\)\(0\)Null0 0 0 0
\(F_{1}\)\(xy\)AND0 0 0 1
\(F_{2}\)\(xy'\)Inhibition0 0 1 0
\(F_{3}\)\(x\)Transfer0 0 1 1
\(F_{4}\)\(x'y\)Inhibition0 1 0 0
\(F_{5}\)\(y\)Transfer0 1 0 1
\(F_{6}\)\(xy'+x'y\)Exclusive-OR0 1 1 0
\(F_{7}\)\(x+y\)OR0 1 1 1
\(F_{8}\)\((x+y)'\)NOR1 0 0 0
\(F_{9}\)\(xy+x'y'\)Equivalence1 0 0 1
\(F_{10}\)\(y'\)Complement1 0 1 0
\(F_{11}\)\(x+y'\)Implication1 0 1 1
\(F_{12}\)\(x'\)Complement1 1 0 0
\(F_{13}\)\(x'+y\)Implication1 1 0 1
\(F_{14}\)\((xy)'\)NAND1 1 1 0
\(F_{15}\)\(1\)Identity1 1 1 1

From slide 5: two functions are constants (\(F_0\), \(F_{15}\)); four are repeated twice (transfer and complement: \(F_3\), \(F_5\), \(F_{10}\), \(F_{12}\)); inhibition and implication are not commutative or associative; the other eight (complement, transfer, AND, OR, NAND, NOR, XOR, equivalence) are the standard gates.

From the past midterms · same skill, exam format
2023 Q29The number of canonical expressions that can be developed over a 3-valued Boolean algebra is
  • (a) 8
  • (b) 16
  • (c) 32
  • (d) 256
  • (e) None
Answer: (d) 256. With 3 variables there are \(2^3=8\) minterms and \(2^{2^3}=2^8=256\) different functions, each with its own canonical expression.
2020 Q30How many input combinations would a truth table have for a six-input AND gate?
  • (a) 32
  • (b) 48
  • (c) 64
  • (d) 128
Answer: (c) 64. \(2^6=64\) rows.

Skill 2 Why NAND and NOR are not associative

Slides 8 to 13

AND and OR gates can be chained in any order. NAND and NOR cannot: a NAND of a NAND is not a three-input NAND.

Method
  1. A 3-input NAND is defined directly as \((xyz)'\).
  2. Chaining two 2-input NANDs gives \(((xy)'z)'\), which is a different function.
  3. To show it, make a truth table with both columns and find a row where they differ. The same holds for NOR.
Worked example · read it, then cover it and redo it on paper
Slides 9 and 13Lec 5

Show that NAND and NOR are not associative: compare \(\overline{A\cdot B\cdot C}\) with \(\overline{\overline{A\cdot B}\cdot C}\), and \(\overline{A+B+C}\) with \(\overline{\overline{A+B}+C}\).

Find: the truth tables

Answer: the columns differ, so a 3-input NAND/NOR cannot be made by chaining 2-input ones

Solution on paper
\(A\)\(B\)\(C\)\(\overline{ABC}\)\(\overline{\overline{AB}\,C}\)\(\overline{A+B+C}\)\(\overline{\overline{A+B}+C}\)
0001110
0011000
0101101
0111000
1001101
1011000
1101101
1110100

An n-input NAND is made from the n-input AND (which is associative) followed by one inversion. The same holds for NOR and OR.

The method, step by step: Row 001 is enough: \(\overline{ABC}=1\) but \(\overline{\overline{AB}\,C}=0\), so the two columns differ (step 3).
From the past midterms · same skill, exam format
2020 Q32All the following gates are associative except:
  • (a) OR gate
  • (b) AND gate
  • (c) NAND gate
  • (d) XNOR gate
Answer: (c) NAND gate. \(((AB)'C)'\ne(A(BC)')'\): NAND (like NOR) is commutative but not associative (Lecture 5, slide 9).

Skill 3 Build any gate from NAND or NOR only

Slides 10, 14, 26 and 27

NAND gates alone (or NOR gates alone) can make NOT, AND and OR, so they can build any circuit. That is why they are called universal gates.

Method

With NAND gates

  1. NOT: tie both inputs together, \((xx)'=x'\). 1 gate.
  2. AND: a NAND followed by a NAND inverter. 2 gates.
  3. OR: invert each input with a NAND, then NAND them: \((x'y')'=x+y\). 3 gates.

With NOR gates

  1. NOT: \((x+x)'=x'\). 1 gate.
  2. OR: a NOR followed by a NOR inverter. 2 gates.
  3. AND: invert each input, then NOR them: \((x'+y')'=xy\). 3 gates.
  1. Two levels of NAND = a sum of products: \([(ABC)'(DE)']'=ABC+DE\). Two levels of NOR = a product of sums.
Worked example · read it, then cover it and redo it on paper
Slides 10 and 14 · UniversalityLec 5

Build NOT, AND and OR using only NAND gates, then using only NOR gates.

Find: the six circuits

Answer: see the drawings

Solution on paper
NOT from NAND
\(F = X'\)
AND from NAND
\(F = XY\)
OR from NAND
\(F = X+Y\)
NOT from NOR
\(F = X'\)
OR from NOR
\(F = X+Y\)
AND from NOR
\(F = XY\)

A NAND with both inputs tied together, or with one input held at 1, is an inverter. A NOR with both inputs tied, or one input held at 0, is an inverter. The slides draw the second gate of “AND from NAND” as a triangle inverter; it is one more NAND gate used as an inverter.

The method, step by step: Each circuit follows one line of the method: NOT is one gate with its inputs tied, AND is two gates, OR is three (steps 1 to 6).
Your turn · solve on paper first, then open the answer
Slides 26 and 27 (Fig. 2.6 and 2.7)Lec 5

Find \((x\downarrow y)\downarrow z\) and \(x\downarrow(y\downarrow z)\), and the output of two NAND gates (inputs A, B, C and D, E) feeding a third NAND gate.

Find: the three expressions

Hint: work from the inside out: \(x\downarrow y=(x+y)'\), then NOR that with \(z\). For the three NAND gates, apply DeMorgan to the output gate.

Answer: \((x+y)z'\); \(x'(y+z)\); \(F=ABC+DE\)

Solution on paper
\[\begin{aligned}(x\downarrow y)\downarrow z &= [(x+y)'+z]' \\[3pt] &= \boxed{(x+y)\,z'}\end{aligned}\]
\[\begin{aligned}x\downarrow(y\downarrow z) &= [x+(y+z)']' \\[3pt] &= \boxed{x'(y+z)}\end{aligned}\]
\[\begin{aligned}F &= [(ABC)'\cdot(DE)']' \\[3pt] &= (ABC)''+(DE)'' && \text{DeMorgan} \\[3pt] &= \boxed{ABC+DE}\end{aligned}\]
From the past midterms · same skill, exam format
2025 Q17The universal gate is ……… new in 2025
  • (a) OR gate
  • (b) NAND gate
  • (c) Both A & B
  • (d) AND gate
  • (e) None
Answer: (b) NAND gate. NAND (and NOR) can build NOT, AND and OR, so any function. OR and AND alone cannot make an inverter. Of the listed gates only NAND is universal.
2023 Q17Which logic function is produced by adding an inverter to each input and to the output of an AND gate?
  • (a) XOR
  • (b) OR
  • (c) NOR
  • (d) NAND
  • (e) XNOR
Answer: (b) OR. \((A'B')'=A+B\) by DeMorgan: an OR gate.
2023 Q19The number of NOR gates required to produce a three-input AND gate is:
  • (a) 4
  • (b) 3
  • (c) 2
  • (d) 1
  • (e) 0
Answer: (a) 4. \(ABC=(A'+B'+C')'\): three NOR gates used as inverters and one 3-input NOR: 4.
2023 Q22Which of the following expressions is not equivalent to \(X'\)?
  • (a) X NAND X
  • (b) X NOR X
  • (c) X NAND 1
  • (d) X NOR 1
  • (e) a & b
Answer: (d) X NOR 1. \(X\) NAND \(X=X'\); \(X\) NOR \(X=X'\); \(X\) NAND \(1=X'\); but \(X\) NOR \(1=(X+1)'=0\).
2020 Q1What is the minimum number of two-input NAND gates used to perform the function of a two-input OR gate?
  • (a) one
  • (b) two
  • (c) three
  • (d) four
Answer: (c) three. \(A+B=(A'B')'\). Two NAND gates make \(A'\) and \(B'\), a third NAND combines them: three gates.

Skill 4 XOR, XNOR, odd and even functions

Slides 15 to 28

XOR is 1 when its inputs differ. Chained over several inputs it becomes the odd function: 1 when the number of 1s is odd. That is exactly what a parity bit needs.

Method
  1. \(x\oplus y=xy'+x'y\). XNOR: \((x\oplus y)'=xy+x'y'\).
  2. Identities: \(x\oplus0=x\), \(x\oplus1=x'\), \(x\oplus x=0\), \(x\oplus x'=1\).
  3. XOR is commutative and associative, so \(x\oplus y\oplus z\) can be built from two 2-input gates in any order.
  4. Odd function: 1 for an odd number of 1s, \(\Sigma(1,2,4,7)\) for three variables. Even function: its complement, \(\Sigma(0,3,5,6)\).
  5. Quick evaluation: count the 1s among the inputs.
Worked example · read it, then cover it and redo it on paper
Slides 21 and 22Lec 5

Check the XOR and XNOR identities, and decide whether chaining two-input XOR / XNOR gates is associative.

Find: the identities and the associativity result

Answer: all identities hold; XOR is associative; chained XNOR gives the odd function, not \(\overline{X\oplus Y\oplus Z}\)

Solution on paper
XORWhyXNORWhy
\(X\oplus0=X\)like OR\(\overline{X\oplus0}=\overline X\)like NOR
\(X\oplus1=\overline X\)like NAND\(\overline{X\oplus1}=X\)like AND
\(X\oplus X=0\)inputs always identical\(\overline{X\oplus X}=1\)inputs always identical
\(X\oplus\overline X=1\)inputs always different\(\overline{X\oplus\overline X}=0\)inputs always different
\(X\)\(Y\)\(Z\)\((X\oplus Y)\oplus Z\)\(X\oplus(Y\oplus Z)\)\((X\odot Y)\odot Z\)\(X\odot(Y\odot Z)\)\(\overline{X\oplus Y\oplus Z}\)

00111110
01011110
01100001
10011110
10100001
11000001
11111110

\(\odot\) here means the two-input XNOR. The two chained XNOR columns are equal to each other and equal to the odd function \(X\oplus Y\oplus Z\); they are the opposite of the even function in the last column. That is the reason the slide warns that the 3-input “even function” must be built as XOR, XOR, then one inverter.

The method, step by step: \(X\oplus1=\overline X\) because XOR with 1 flips the other input (step 2). In the table, \((X\oplus Y)\oplus Z\) and \(X\oplus(Y\oplus Z)\) give the same column, so XOR is associative (step 3).
Your turn · solve on paper first, then open the answer
Slides 23, 24 and 28Lec 5

Write the odd function \(X\oplus Y\oplus Z\) and the even function as sums of minterms, give the truth table, and draw the odd function with 2-input XOR gates.

Find: expressions, truth table, circuit

Hint: list the rows of X, Y, Z that have an odd number of 1s: 001, 010, 100, 111.

Answer: odd \(=\Sigma(1,2,4,7)\); even \(=\Sigma(0,3,5,6)\)

Solution on paper
\[\begin{aligned}X\oplus Y\oplus Z &= \overline X\,\overline YZ+\overline XY\overline Z+X\overline Y\,\overline Z+XYZ \\[3pt] &= \boxed{\Sigma(1,2,4,7)}\end{aligned}\]
\[\begin{aligned}\overline{X\oplus Y\oplus Z} &= \overline X\,\overline Y\,\overline Z+\overline XYZ+X\overline YZ+XY\overline Z \\[3pt] &= \boxed{\Sigma(0,3,5,6)}\end{aligned}\]

Use of the odd function: its output is the even-parity bit for the three inputs, and an even-parity checker gives 1 when there is an error.

Slide 20Lec 5

Show that the output of the top gate of the NAND-only XOR circuit is \(X\overline Y\).

Top gate inputs\(X\) and \(\overline{XY}\)

Find: the algebra

Hint: \(X\cdot\overline{XY}=X(\overline X+\overline Y)\) by DeMorgan; then \(X\overline X=0\).

Answer: \(X\cdot\overline{XY}=X\overline Y\)

Solution on paper
\[\begin{aligned}X\cdot\overline{XY} &= X(\overline X+\overline Y) && \text{DeMorgan} \\[3pt] &= X\overline X+X\overline Y \\[3pt] &= 0+X\overline Y \\[3pt] &= \boxed{X\overline Y}\end{aligned}\]

The full circuit uses four NAND gates: \(N_1=\overline{XY}\), \(N_2=\overline{X N_1}\), \(N_3=\overline{Y N_1}\), output \(=\overline{N_2N_3}=X\overline Y+\overline XY=X\oplus Y\).

From the past midterms · same skill, exam format
2023 Q15Odd parity of a word can be conveniently tested by
  • (a) XOR
  • (b) AND
  • (c) OR
  • (d) NAND
  • (e) XNOR
Answer: (a) XOR. The XOR (odd function) of all bits is 1 when the number of 1s is odd, so a chain of XOR gates tests parity.
2023 Q41In the circuit of the given figure, Y = (three XOR gates in a chain; the input x feeds both inputs of the first gate and the second input of the second and third gates)
  • (a) 0
  • (b) 1
  • (c) x
  • (d) x'
  • (e) None
Answer: (a) 0. First gate: \(x\oplus x=0\). Second gate: \(0\oplus x=x\). Third gate: \(x\oplus x=0\). \(Y=0\).
2020 Q19Which one of the following logic expressions is incorrect?
  • (a) \(1\oplus0=1\)
  • (b) \(1\oplus1\oplus0=1\)
  • (c) \(1\oplus1\oplus1=1\)
  • (d) \(1\oplus1=0\)
Answer: (b) \(1\oplus1\oplus0=1\). XOR is 1 for an odd number of 1s. \(1\oplus1\oplus0\) has two 1s, so it equals 0, not 1.

Skill 5 Positive and negative logic

Slides 29 to 34

A real gate works with two voltages, H and L. Which one is called 1 is a choice, and changing the choice turns every gate into its dual.

Method
  1. Positive logic: H = 1, L = 0. Negative logic: H = 0, L = 1.
  2. Rewrite the H / L table both ways and recognise the gate.
  3. Result to remember: AND ↔ OR and NAND ↔ NOR.
Worked example · read it, then cover it and redo it on paper
Slides 29 to 34Lec 5

A gate's behaviour is given with voltage levels H and L. Read the same table in positive logic (H = 1) and in negative logic (H = 0). What does each gate become?

Example tablez = H only when x = H and y = H

Find: the pairs of equivalent gates

Answer: AND ↔ OR, NAND ↔ NOR

Solution on paper
xyzpositive logic (H=1): x y znegative logic (H=0): x y z
LLL0 0 01 1 1
LHL0 1 01 0 1
HLL1 0 00 1 1
HHH1 1 10 0 0

Positive reading: output 1 only for 1 1 → AND. Negative reading: output 0 only for 0 0 → OR. The same piece of hardware is a positive-logic AND gate and a negative-logic OR gate.

Same hardware read asis also a
positive-logic ANDnegative-logic OR
positive-logic ORnegative-logic AND
positive-logic NANDnegative-logic NOR
positive-logic NORnegative-logic NAND
The method, step by step: The positive reading gives an output of 1 only for 1 1, which is AND. The negative reading of the same table gives an output of 0 only for 0 0, which is OR (steps 2 and 3).
From the past midterms · same skill, exam format
2025 Q18A positive OR gate is also a negative ___ gate new in 2025
  • (a) NAND
  • (b) OR
  • (c) NOR
  • (d) AND
  • (e) None
Answer: (d) AND. Changing from positive to negative logic turns a gate into its dual: a positive-logic OR is a negative-logic AND (Lecture 5, slide 34).
2020 Q22A positive NAND gate is also a negative:
  • (a) NAND
  • (b) NOR
  • (c) AND
  • (d) OR
Answer: (b) NOR. Swapping H = 1 for H = 0 turns every gate into its dual: positive NAND ↔ negative NOR (Lecture 5, slide 34).
2021 Q22The relationship between a NOR gate and a negative-AND gate is expressed by
  • (a) \(AB=\overline{A+B}\)
  • (b) \(\overline{A+B}=\overline A\,\overline B\)
  • (c) \(\overline A+\overline B=\overline{A+B}\)
  • (d) \(AB=\overline{\overline A}+\overline{\overline B}\)
Answer: (b) \(\overline{A+B}=\overline A\,\overline B\). A negative-AND gate is an AND gate with inverted inputs, \(\overline A\,\overline B\). By DeMorgan this equals the NOR, \(\overline{A+B}\). (The options are small in the scan; (b) is read as this identity.)

Formula sheet for revision

Everything above in one place, with the doctor's steps and the traps. Revise from it once you can do the skills; it is hard to learn from it the first time.

Rules to memorise
  • \(n\) variables: \(2^{\,n}\) rows in the truth table, \(2^{2^{\,n}}\) functions. For \(n=2\): 4 rows, 16 functions.
  • Standard gates: inverter, buffer, AND, OR, NAND, NOR, XOR, XNOR (equivalence).
  • NAND, two symbols: AND-invert \((xyz)'\) = invert-OR \(x'+y'+z'\).
  • NOR, two symbols: OR-invert \((x+y+z)'\) = invert-AND \(x'y'z'\).
  • From NAND only: NOT \(=(xx)'\) (inputs tied, or one input tied to 1); AND = NAND + NAND-inverter; OR = invert both inputs, then NAND. Gate counts: NOT 1, AND 2, OR 3.
  • From NOR only: NOT \(=(x+x)'\) (inputs tied, or one input tied to 0); OR = NOR + NOR-inverter; AND = invert both inputs, then NOR. Gate counts: NOT 1, OR 2, AND 3.
  • AND and OR are commutative and associative. NAND and NOR are commutative but not associative: \((x\downarrow y)\downarrow z=(x+y)z'\) but \(x\downarrow(y\downarrow z)=x'(y+z)\). A 3-input NAND is defined directly as \((xyz)'\).
  • Cascaded NAND gates (NAND–NAND) = sum of products: \([(ABC)'(DE)']'=ABC+DE\). Cascaded NOR gates (NOR–NOR) = product of sums.
  • XOR: \(x\oplus y=xy'+x'y\). XNOR: \((x\oplus y)'=xy+x'y'\).
  • XOR identities: \(x\oplus0=x\), \(x\oplus1=x'\), \(x\oplus x=0\), \(x\oplus x'=1\), \(x\oplus y'=x'\oplus y=(x\oplus y)'\). XOR is commutative and associative.
  • Odd function \(x\oplus y\oplus z=x'y'z+x'yz'+xy'z'+xyz\) = 1 for an odd number of 1s (used to make and check even parity). Even function = its complement (used for odd parity).
  • Positive logic: H = 1, L = 0. Negative logic: H = 0, L = 1. Changing the logic turns a gate into its dual: AND ↔ OR, NAND ↔ NOR.
Traps
  • Calling a 3-input XOR “XOR” in a theory answer. The slides call it the odd function; XOR and XNOR symbols are defined for two inputs.
  • Slide 22 says XNOR is not associative. For the two-input operation, \((x\odot y)\odot z\) and \(x\odot(y\odot z)\) do give the same truth table (see the card below); what the slide stresses is that \(\overline{x\oplus y\oplus z}\) is not obtained by chaining XNOR gates. Slide 28 itself says “XOR and XNOR gates are commutative and associative”. In an exam MCQ use the lecture's statement: the gate named “not associative” is NAND (or NOR).
  • Inhibition (\(xy'\)) and implication (\(x'+y\)) are neither commutative nor associative, so they are not standard gates.
  • Forgetting that a NAND drawn with bubbles on its inputs and an OR body is still a NAND.
← Lecture 4 · Canonical formsLecture 6 · Karnaugh maps →

Lecture 6 · Karnaugh maps and NAND / NOR implementation

Source: “ELE121_Lec 6.pdf” (58 slides, “Lec 6: Karnaugh Map”). Chapter 3: map method, prime implicants, product-of-sums simplification, don't-care conditions, NAND and NOR implementation, other two-level forms.

In plain words

A Karnaugh map (K-map) is the truth table redrawn as a grid, one square per minterm. The rows and columns are numbered in Gray order (00, 01, 11, 10) so that squares that touch differ in exactly one variable. The left and right edges touch each other, and so do the top and bottom edges.

To simplify: put a 1 in the square of every minterm, then circle the 1s in rectangles of 1, 2, 4, 8 or 16 squares, as big as possible. Each circle becomes one product term, and the variable that changes inside a circle disappears. Bigger circles mean shorter terms; fewer circles mean fewer gates.

How to study this lecture

The lecture is 8 skills, in order. For each skill: read the idea and the method, follow the worked example, then solve the Your turn questions on paper before you open the answer. Tick the box at the end of a skill when you can solve its questions without help.

  1. Two- and three-variable K-maps
  2. Four-variable K-maps
  3. Prime and essential prime implicants
  4. Five-variable K-maps
  5. Product of sums from a map
  6. Don't-care conditions
  7. NAND and NOR circuits
  8. Other two-level forms

Exam weight: K-maps, don't cares and two-level forms: 15 of the 143 multiple-choice questions in the four past midterms. The written question of 2025 ends with a NAND circuit (Skill 7).

Already know it? Jump to the formula sheet, or to revise.

Skill 1 Two- and three-variable K-maps

Slides 3 to 12

A Karnaugh map is a truth table laid out so that neighbouring squares differ in one variable only. Circling neighbouring 1s removes that variable, so the map simplifies by picture.

Method
  1. Draw the map with the variable names and the labels 00, 01, 11, 10 (this order, not 00, 01, 10, 11).
  2. Write a 1 in each minterm square.
  3. Circle groups of 1, 2, 4 or 8 squares that form rectangles. The left and right edges are neighbours.
  4. Make the groups as large as possible and use as few as possible. Every 1 must be in at least one group.
  5. For each group keep only the variables that do not change inside it: value 1 → \(x\), value 0 → \(x'\).
  6. OR the terms together.
Worked example · read it, then cover it and redo it on paper
Slide 5 (Fig. 3.2)Lec 6

Show the functions \(xy\) and \(x+y\) on two-variable maps.

Find: the maps

Answer: \(xy=m_3\); \(x+y=m_1+m_2+m_3\)

Solution on paper
The method, step by step: \(xy\) is 1 only in square 11, and \(x+y\) fills squares 01, 10 and 11 (step 2).

Three-variable map (slides 6 and 7): squares \(m_0\) and \(m_2\) are adjacent through the edge, \(m_0+m_2=x'y'z'+x'yz'=x'z'(y'+y)=x'z'\); likewise \(m_4+m_6=xz'\) and \(m_5+m_7=xz\).

Worked example · read it, then cover it and redo it on paper
Slide 8Lec 6

Simplify \(F(x,y,z)=\Sigma(1, 2, 3, 5, 7)\) with a Karnaugh map.

Minterms (cells with 1)1, 2, 3, 5, 7

Find: the minimal sum of products

Answer: \(F = z + x'y\)

Solution on paper

Each outlined group gives one product term: keep only the variables that do not change inside the group.

\[F=\boxed{z + x'y}\]
The method, step by step: The four 1s with \(z=1\) form one group, in which only \(z\) stays the same: \(z\) (steps 3 and 5). The 1 in square 010 pairs with square 011 to give \(x'y\) (step 4).

Answer: \(F = z' + xy + x'y'\)

\[F=\boxed{x'y' + z' + xy}\]

Example 3.4Lec 6

Let \(F=A'C+A'B+AB'C+BC\). (a) Express it as a sum of minterms. (b) Find the minimal sum of products.

Minterms (cells with 1)1, 2, 3, 5, 7

Find: the minimal sum of products

Hint: first turn each term into minterms, or fill the map term by term, then group.

Answer: \(F = C + A'B\)

Solution on paper

Each outlined group gives one product term: keep only the variables that do not change inside the group.

\[F=\boxed{C + A'B}\]

(a) Mark each term on the map: \(A'C\) → 1, 3; \(A'B\) → 2, 3; \(AB'C\) → 5; \(BC\) → 3, 7. So \(F=\Sigma(1,2,3,5,7)\).

Answer: (a) 8. The map is practical only for fewer than 7 variables; of the listed values, 8 is the one where it is not convenient.

Skill 2 Four-variable K-maps

Slides 13 to 19

The same rules on a 4 × 4 map. Now the top and bottom edges are neighbours too, and the four corners form one group.

Method
  1. Rows and columns both go 00, 01, 11, 10.
  2. Groups of 1, 2, 4, 8 or 16 squares. A group of \(2^{\,k}\) squares gives a term with \(4-k\) literals.
  3. Start with the 1s that can be grouped in only one way.
  4. Use the wrap-around: left / right edges, top / bottom edges, and the four corners.
  5. Write one term per group and check that every 1 is covered.
Worked example · read it, then cover it and redo it on paper
Example 3.5Lec 6

Simplify \(F(w,x,y,z)=\Sigma(0, 1, 2, 4, 5, 6, 8, 9, 12, 13, 14)\) with a Karnaugh map.

Minterms (cells with 1)0, 1, 2, 4, 5, 6, 8, 9, 12, 13, 14

Find: the minimal sum of products

Answer: \(F = y' + w'z' + xz'\)

Solution on paper

Each outlined group gives one product term: keep only the variables that do not change inside the group.

\[F=\boxed{y' + w'z' + xz'}\]
The method, step by step: The eight squares with \(y=0\) form one group, \(y'\): 8 squares → 1 literal (step 2). The remaining 1s in squares 2, 6 and 14 join neighbours through the edges in two groups of four, \(w'z'\) and \(xz'\) (step 4).
Your turn · solve on paper first, then open the answer
Example 3.6Lec 6

Simplify \(F=A'B'C'+B'CD'+A'BCD'+AB'C'\).

Minterms (cells with 1)0, 1, 2, 6, 8, 9, 10

Find: the minimal sum of products

Hint: first turn each term into minterms (0, 1, 2, 6, 8, 9, 10). The four corners 0, 2, 8, 10 form one group.

Answer: \(F = B'D' + B'C' + A'CD'\)

Solution on paper

Each outlined group gives one product term: keep only the variables that do not change inside the group.

\[F=\boxed{B'C' + A'CD' + B'D'}\]

\(B'D'\) is the group of the four corners.

Slide 18Lec 6

Simplify \(F(W,X,Y,Z)=\Sigma(0, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 10, 13, 15)\) with a Karnaugh map.

Minterms (cells with 1)0, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 10, 13, 15

Find: the minimal sum of products

Hint: look for the large groups first: the answer has four groups of four.

Answer: \(F = XZ + W'Y + X'Z' + W'X\)

Solution on paper

Each outlined group gives one product term: keep only the variables that do not change inside the group.

\[F=\boxed{XZ + W'Y + X'Z' + W'X}\]

\(X'Z'\) is the four-corner group. Minterms 4 can be covered by \(W'X\) (the slide's choice) or by \(W'Z'\); both answers have 8 literals. The canonical form has \(11\times4=44\) literals, which answers the slide's “L = ?”.

Slide 19Lec 6

Simplify \(F(W,X,Y,Z)=\Sigma_m(3,4,5,7,9,13,14,15)\). The slide asks: what is the best way to handle the 1 in square 9?

Minterms3, 4, 5, 7, 9, 13, 14, 15

Find: the minimal sum of products

Hint: square 9 has only one neighbouring 1 (square 13), so that pair has to be one of the groups.

Answer: \(F = W'YZ + W'XY' + WY'Z + WXY\)

Solution on paper

Square 9 has only one neighbour that holds a 1, square 13. So it must be grouped as \(W\overline YZ\) (squares 9 and 13). After the four pairs are drawn, every 1 is covered, and the centre group of four, \(XZ\) (squares 5, 7, 13, 15), is redundant: each of its squares is already inside one of the pairs.

\[F=\boxed{W'YZ + W'XY' + WY'Z + WXY}\]

The expression printed at the bottom of the slide, \(ZX+\overline WYZ+WXY+\overline WX\overline Y\), does not cover minterm 9, and it keeps the redundant term \(ZX\). This map is the standard warning: do not start with the biggest group; start with the 1s that have only one possible group.

From the past midterms · same skill, exam format
2021 Q23The Boolean equation ________ results from this Karnaugh map. Rows \(\overline A\,\overline B\): 0 0; \(\overline AB\): 1 1; \(AB\): 1 1; \(A\overline B\): 0 1 (columns \(\overline C\), C).
  • (a) \(AB+A'B\)
  • (b) \(AB'\)
  • (c) \(AC+B\)
  • (d) \(ABC+ABC'+AB'C\)
Answer: (c) \(AC+B\). The four 1s of the two middle rows form the group B. The 1 at \(A\overline BC\) pairs with the one above it (\(ABC\)) to give \(AC\). \(X=B+AC\).
2021 Q33Which of the K-maps in the figure represents the expression \(X=AC+BC+B\)? Each map lists the rows \(\overline A\,\overline B\), \(\overline AB\), \(AB\), \(A\overline B\) with the columns \(\overline C\), C. Map a: 11 / 11 / 00 / 00. Map b: 01 / 00 / 11 / 11. Map c: 00 / 11 / 11 / 01. Map d: 11 / 01 / 01 / 11.
  • (a) b
  • (b) d
  • (c) a
  • (d) c
Answer: (d) c. \(X=AC+BC+B=B+AC\) (BC is absorbed by B). B fills the rows \(\overline AB\) and \(AB\) with 1s; AC adds the square \(A\overline BC\). That is map c.

Skill 3 Prime and essential prime implicants

Slides 20 to 34

When several groupings look possible, these two ideas decide which groups you must take and which are a free choice.

Method
  1. Prime implicant: a group that cannot be made any bigger. List them all.
  2. Essential prime implicant: a prime implicant that covers a 1 that no other prime implicant covers.
  3. Take every essential prime implicant first.
  4. Cover the 1s that are left with as few prime implicants as possible.
  5. There may be more than one equally good answer.
Worked example · read it, then cover it and redo it on paper
Slides 25 and 28 (Fig. 3.11)Lec 6

For \(F(A,B,C,D)=\Sigma(0,2,3,5,7,8,9,10,11,13,15)\) find all prime implicants, the essential ones, and every minimal expression.

Minterms0, 2, 3, 5, 7, 8, 9, 10, 11, 13, 15

Find: prime implicants, essential prime implicants, simplified forms

Answer: six prime implicants; essential: \(BD\) and \(B'D'\); four equally good answers

Solution on paper

After the two essential groups, squares 3, 9 and 11 are still uncovered. Square 3 needs \(CD\) or \(B'C\); square 9 needs \(AD\) or \(AB'\); square 11 is inside all four. Two choices × two choices:

\[\begin{aligned}F &= B'D' + BD + B'C + AB' \\[3pt] &= B'D' + BD + B'C + AD \\[3pt] &= B'D' + BD + CD + AB' \\[3pt] &= B'D' + BD + CD + AD\end{aligned}\]

All four have 4 terms and 8 literals, as listed on the slide.

The method, step by step: Squares 0 and 5 each lie in only one prime implicant, so \(B'D'\) and \(BD\) are essential (steps 2 and 3). Squares 3, 9 and 11 are left: \(CD\) or \(B'C\) covers 3, \(AD\) or \(AB'\) covers 9, giving four equally good answers (steps 4 and 5).
Your turn · solve on paper first, then open the answer
Slide 26Lec 6

Find all possible prime implicants of \(G(A,B,C,D)=\Sigma_m(0,2,3,4,7,12,13,14,15)\). Hint on the slide: there are seven. (The slide names the variables W, X, Y, Z on the map.)

Minterms0, 2, 3, 4, 7, 12, 13, 14, 15

Find: the seven prime implicants

Hint: look for every group that cannot grow; the slide says there are seven. Remember the corners and the edge wrap-around.

Answer: \(AB\), \(A'B'D'\), \(A'C'D'\), \(A'B'C\), \(A'CD\), \(BC'D'\), \(BCD\)

Solution on paper
Prime implicantSquaresEssential?
\(AB\)12, 13, 14, 15yes
\(A'B'D'\)0, 2no
\(A'C'D'\)0, 4no
\(A'B'C\)2, 3no
\(A'CD\)3, 7no
\(BC'D'\)4, 12no
\(BCD\)7, 15no

Only \(AB\) is essential (square 13 has no other group). A minimal cover needs \(AB\) plus three of the pairs, for example \(AB + A'B'D' + A'C'D' + A'CD\).

Slide 34 · Multiple optimal solutionsLec 6

A map has the six prime implicants \(A'B'C'\), \(A'C'D\), \(A'BD\), \(BCD\), \(ABC\), \(ACD'\). Which are essential, and what are the optimal solutions?

Function\(\Sigma(0, 1, 5, 7, 10, 14, 15)\)

Find: essential set and optimal covers

Hint: a prime implicant is essential if it holds a 1 that no other one holds. Check squares 0 and 10 first.

Answer: essential: \(A'B'C'\), \(ACD'\); three optimal solutions

Solution on paper
\[\begin{aligned}F &= A'B'C' + ACD' + A'C'D + BCD \\[3pt] &= A'B'C' + ACD' + A'BD + BCD \\[3pt] &= A'B'C' + ACD' + A'BD + ABC\end{aligned}\]

The non-essential prime implicants are \(A'C'D\), \(A'BD\), \(BCD\), \(ABC\); two of them are needed to finish the cover, and three pairs work.

Slides 21 and 27 show small three-variable maps with their results. Each result was recomputed:

Skill 4 Five-variable K-maps

Slides 30 to 33

Five variables need two 4-variable maps, one for \(A=0\) and one for \(A=1\), imagined one on top of the other.

Method
  1. Draw two 4-variable maps for \(B, C, D, E\): the first for \(A=0\) (squares 0 to 15), the second for \(A=1\) (16 to 31).
  2. Group inside each map as usual.
  3. Squares in the same position of the two maps are neighbours. A group that uses both maps drops \(A\).
  4. A group of \(2^{\,k}\) squares gives \(5-k\) literals.
Worked example · read it, then cover it and redo it on paper
Example 3.7Lec 6

Simplify \(F(A,B,C,D,E)=\Sigma(0, 2, 4, 6, 9, 13, 21, 23, 25, 29, 31)\) with a Karnaugh map.

Minterms (cells with 1)0, 2, 4, 6, 9, 13, 21, 23, 25, 29, 31

Find: the minimal sum of products

Answer: \(F = A'B'E' + BD'E + ACE\)

Solution on paper

Each outlined group gives one product term: keep only the variables that do not change inside the group.

\[F=\boxed{A'B'E' + BD'E + ACE}\]

A five-variable map is two four-variable maps, one for \(A=0\) and one for \(A=1\), imagined one on top of the other. Squares in the same position of the two maps are adjacent. \(BD'E\) is a group of four made of two squares in each map (9, 13 and 25, 29), so A drops out.

The method, step by step: \(BD'E\) uses squares 9 and 13 in the \(A=0\) map and 25 and 29 in the same places of the \(A=1\) map, so \(A\) drops out (step 3).
K (group of \(2^K\) squares)squaresn = 2n = 3n = 4n = 5

5320

Slide 30: number of literals in a term for a group of \(2^K\) adjacent squares in an n-variable map.

Skill 5 Product of sums from a map

Slides 35 to 39

Grouping the 0s instead of the 1s gives \(F'\). Complementing it with DeMorgan gives \(F\) as a product of sums.

Method
  1. Fill the map with the 1s of \(F\); every other square is a 0.
  2. Group the 0s, as large as possible. This gives \(F'\) as a sum of products.
  3. Apply DeMorgan, \(F=(F')'\): each product becomes a sum with every literal flipped.
  4. Circuit: OR gates first, then one AND gate.
Worked example · read it, then cover it and redo it on paper
Example 3.8Lec 6, slides 36 and 37

Simplify \(F(A,B,C,D)=\Sigma(0,1,2,5,8,9,10)\) into (a) sum-of-products form and (b) product-of-sums form, and draw both.

Minterms0, 1, 2, 5, 8, 9, 10
Zeros3, 4, 6, 7, 11, 12, 13, 14, 15

Find: SOP, POS and the two circuits

Answer: (a) \(F=B'D'+B'C'+A'C'D\)   (b) \(F=(A'+B')(C'+D')(B'+D)\)

Solution on paper
\[\begin{aligned}F &= \boxed{B'D'+B'C'+A'C'D}\end{aligned}\]
\[\begin{aligned}F' &= CD + BD' + AB\end{aligned}\]
\[\begin{aligned}F &= (CD + BD' + AB)' && \text{DeMorgan} \\[3pt] &= \boxed{(A'+B')(C'+D')(B'+D)}\end{aligned}\]
The method, step by step: Grouping the 0s gives \(F'=CD+BD'+AB\) (step 2). DeMorgan turns each product into a sum of flipped literals: \((C'+D')(B'+D)(A'+B')\) (step 3).
Your turn · solve on paper first, then open the answer
Slides 38 and 39 (Table 3.2)Lec 6

The function of Table 3.2 is 1 for rows 1, 3, 4, 6. Write it as a sum of minterms and as a product of maxterms, and simplify by combining the 1s, then by combining the 0s.

F = 1 at1, 3, 4, 6
F = 0 at0, 2, 5, 7

Find: \(F\) in SOP and POS

Hint: the 1s at 1, 3, 4, 6 group into \(x'z\) and \(xz'\). For the product of sums, group the 0s at 0, 2, 5, 7 and apply DeMorgan.

Answer: \(F=\Sigma(1,3,4,6)=\Pi(0,2,5,7)\); \(F=x'z+xz'=(x'+z')(x+z)\)

Solution on paper
\[\begin{aligned}F &= (xz+x'z')' \\[3pt] &= \boxed{(x'+z')(x+z)}\end{aligned}\]

\(F=x'z+xz'=x\oplus z\): the output does not depend on y.

Don't-care conditions

Skill 6 Don't-care conditions

Slides 40 to 42

Some input combinations never happen, so the output there does not matter. Marked X, they may be used as 1s whenever that makes a group bigger.

Method
  1. Put the 1s and the Xs on the map.
  2. Treat an X as a 1 only where it enlarges a group; otherwise treat it as a 0.
  3. Every 1 must be covered. An X never has to be.
  4. Different choices can give different answers that are equally correct.
Worked example · read it, then cover it and redo it on paper
Example 3.9Lec 6, slides 40 and 41

Simplify \(F(w,x,y,z)=\Sigma(1,3,7,11,15)\) which has the don't-care conditions \(d(w,x,y,z)=\Sigma(0,2,5)\).

1s1, 3, 7, 11, 15
Don't cares0, 2, 5

Find: the simplified function

Answer: \(F=yz+w'x'\) or \(F=yz+w'z\) (either is acceptable)

Solution on paper

An X is used only when it makes a group bigger. The two answers are different functions (they differ on the don't-care rows), but both agree with the required 1s and 0s.

The method, step by step: \(yz\) covers 3, 7, 11 and 15. Square 1 still needs a group: with the Xs in 0 and 2 it forms \(w'x'\), or with the X in 5 it forms \(w'z\) (steps 2 to 4).
From the past midterms · same skill, exam format
2023 Q21Simplify \(F(w,x,y,z)=\Sigma(2,3,9,11,15)\) which has the don't-care conditions \(d(w,x,y,z)=\Sigma(0,2,5,7,13)\).
  • (a) \(w'x'+wz\)
  • (b) \(yz+x'z\)
  • (c) \(wz+x'z\)
  • (d) a & b & c
  • (e) a & b
Answer: (d) a & b & c. With \(F=\Sigma(1,3,9,11,15)\) and \(d=\Sigma(0,2,5,7,13)\), each of the three expressions covers all five 1s and touches no 0 (they differ only on don't-care squares), so all three are acceptable.
Check: As printed, minterm 2 is listed both in F and in the don't cares, and with a 1 required at square 2 and a 0 at square 1 none of (a), (b), (c) fits. The student's working, and the pattern of Example 3.9, use \(F=\Sigma(1,3,9,11,15)\); the first minterm is a misprint for 1.

Skill 7 NAND and NOR circuits

Slides 43 to 51

Every sum-of-products circuit can be built from NAND gates only, and every product-of-sums circuit from NOR gates only, with exactly the same wiring.

Method

NAND–NAND

  1. Simplify \(F\) to a sum of products.
  2. One NAND gate per product term, with that term's literals as its inputs.
  3. One NAND gate at the output, fed by all the first-level gates.
  4. A term with a single literal goes into the output gate complemented, or through an inverter.

NOR–NOR

  1. Simplify to a product of sums, use one NOR gate per sum term and one NOR gate at the output.

Multilevel circuits

  1. Draw the AND–OR circuit, replace every gate with a NAND, and add an inverter wherever a bubble is left without a partner.
Worked example · read it, then cover it and redo it on paper
Slide 44 (Fig. 3.20)Lec 6

Implement \(F=AB+CD\) with NAND gates.

Find: the two-level NAND circuit

Answer: \(F=((AB)'(CD)')'\)

Solution on paper
\[\begin{aligned}F &= ((AB+CD)')' && \text{double complement} \\[3pt] &= \boxed{((AB)'\,(CD)')'} && \text{DeMorgan inside}\end{aligned}\]
The method, step by step: \(F=AB+CD\) is already a sum of products. A double complement and DeMorgan give \(((AB)'(CD)')'\): two first-level NANDs and one output NAND (steps 2 and 3).
Worked example · read it, then cover it and redo it on paper
Example 3.10Lec 6, slide 45

Implement \(F(x,y,z)=\Sigma(1,2,3,4,5,7)\) with NAND gates.

Minterms1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 7

Find: simplified SOP and the NAND circuit

Answer: \(F=xy'+x'y+z\); three NAND gates, with \(z'\) fed straight to the output gate

Solution on paper
\[\begin{aligned}F &= \boxed{xy'+x'y+z}\end{aligned}\]
Procedure with two levels of NAND (slide 46)
  1. Simplify to a sum of products.
  2. Draw one NAND gate for each product term; its inputs are the literals of the term.
  3. Draw one NAND gate at the output fed by all first-level gates.
  4. A term with a single literal needs an inverter at the first level (or is fed complemented).
The method, step by step: The map gives \(xy'+x'y+z\) (step 1). Two NANDs make \(xy'\) and \(x'y\), and \(z\) goes into the output NAND as \(z'\) (step 4).
Your turn · solve on paper first, then open the answer
Slide 47 (Fig. 3.22)Lec 6

Implement the multilevel function \(F=A(CD+B)+BC'\) with NAND gates.

Find: AND–OR circuit and NAND circuit

Hint: draw the AND–OR (or OR–AND) circuit first, then change every gate into a NAND (or NOR) and add an inverter wherever a bubble is left without a partner.

Answer: see the drawings

Solution on paper

Rule from the slide: convert every AND to NAND (AND + bubble at the output) and every OR to NAND (bubbles at the inputs). Every bubble that is not cancelled by another bubble on the same wire needs an inverter, or the complemented input literal.

Slide 48 (Fig. 3.23)Lec 6

Implement \(F=(AB'+A'B)(C+D')\) with NAND gates.

Find: the NAND circuit

Hint: draw the AND–OR (or OR–AND) circuit first, then change every gate into a NAND (or NOR) and add an inverter wherever a bubble is left without a partner.

Answer: four NAND gates and an output inverter

Solution on paper
Slides 50 and 51 (Fig. 3.26 and 3.27)Lec 6

Implement \(F=(A+B)(C+D)E\) and \(F=(AB'+A'B)(C+D')\) with NOR gates.

Find: the two NOR circuits

Hint: draw the AND–OR (or OR–AND) circuit first, then change every gate into a NAND (or NOR) and add an inverter wherever a bubble is left without a partner.

Answer: see the drawings

Solution on paper

The NOR function is the dual of the NAND function, so the procedure is the dual: start from a product of sums.

From the past midterms · same skill, exam format
2023 Q13What is the minimum number of NAND gates required to implement the function after simplification, assuming that a variable and its complement are not available: \(F=A+AB'+B'C\)
  • (a) 0
  • (b) 6
  • (c) 2
  • (d) 3
  • (e) 4
Answer: (e) 4. \(F=A+AB'+B'C=A+B'C=(A'\cdot(B'C)')'\). Gates: one NAND as inverter for A, one for B, NAND(B′, C), and the output NAND: 4.
2023 Q8Sum of product (SOP) can be implemented by
  • (a) Cascaded OR
  • (b) Cascaded NOR
  • (c) Cascaded AND
  • (d) Cascaded NAND
  • (e) None
Answer: (d) Cascaded NAND. NAND–NAND (cascaded NAND gates) is the same as AND–OR, a sum of products (Lecture 5, slide 27).
2023 Q7Using the transformation method, you can realize any POS realization of OR-AND with only
  • (a) NOR
  • (b) NAND
  • (c) AND
  • (d) XOR
  • (e) All
Answer: (a) NOR. A product of sums (OR–AND) converts to NOR–NOR. NOR is universal, so NOR gates alone are enough.
2020 Q7What is the minimum number of 2-input NAND gates required to implement \(F=(x'+y')(z+w)\)?
  • (a) 6
  • (b) 5
  • (c) 4
  • (d) 3
Answer: (c) 4. \(x'+y'=(xy)'\) is one NAND; call it a. Then \(F=a(z+w)=az+aw\), a sum of products: NAND(a, z), NAND(a, w) and one output NAND. Total 4.
2021 Q16What is the minimum number of NAND gates required to implement the function after simplification: \(F=A+AB'+AB'C\)
  • (a) 4
  • (b) 5
  • (c) 6
  • (d) Zero
Answer: (d) Zero. \(A+AB'+AB'C=A(1+B'+B'C)=A\). The output is the input A itself; no gate is needed.

Skill 8 Other two-level forms

Slides 52 to 57

There are 16 ways to put two levels of gates together. Eight of them collapse into a single operation (degenerate); the other eight are useful, in four pairs.

Method
  1. AND–OR = NAND–NAND: sum of products.
  2. OR–AND = NOR–NOR: product of sums.
  3. AND–NOR = NAND–AND (AND-OR-INVERT): \(F=(AB+CD+E)'\).
  4. OR–NAND = NOR–OR (OR-AND-INVERT): \(F=[(A+B)(C+D)E]'\).
  5. Degenerate: AND–AND, AND–NAND, OR–OR, OR–NOR, NAND–OR, NAND–NOR, NOR–AND, NOR–NAND.

Wired logic (slide 52): joining the outputs of two open-collector TTL NAND gates gives a wired-AND, \(F=(AB)'\cdot(CD)'=(AB+CD)'\), the AND-OR-INVERT function. Joining the outputs of two ECL NOR gates gives a wired-OR, \(F=(A+B)'+(C+D)'=[(A+B)(C+D)]'\), the OR-AND-INVERT function.

Slides 53 to 57: the 16 ways to combine two levels of AND, OR, NAND, NOR gates (first level – second level). Computed with two first-level gates on inputs A, B and C, D:

FormTypeImplements
AND–ANDdegenerate: a single AND
AND–ORnon-degeneratesum of products
AND–NANDdegenerate: a single NAND
AND–NORnon-degenerateAND-OR-INVERT
OR–ANDnon-degenerateproduct of sums
OR–ORdegenerate: a single OR
OR–NANDnon-degenerateOR-AND-INVERT
OR–NORdegenerate: a single NOR
NAND–ANDnon-degenerateAND-OR-INVERT
NAND–ORdegenerate: a single NAND
NAND–NANDnon-degeneratesum of products
NAND–NORdegenerate: a single AND
NOR–ANDdegenerate: a single NOR
NOR–ORnon-degenerateOR-AND-INVERT
NOR–NANDdegenerate: a single OR
NOR–NORnon-degenerateproduct of sums
Worked example · read it, then cover it and redo it on paper
Slides 55 and 57 (Fig. 3.29 and 3.30)Lec 6

Draw the AND-OR-INVERT circuit for \(F=(AB+CD+E)'\) and the OR-AND-INVERT circuit for \(F=[(A+B)(C+D)E]'\).

Find: one circuit of each kind

Answer: AND–NOR and OR–NAND

Solution on paper
The method, step by step: \(F'=AB+CD+E\) is a sum of products, so \(F\) is built as AND gates feeding a NOR (step 3). The OR-AND-INVERT circuit is its dual (step 4).
From the past midterms · same skill, exam format
2023 Q28The NOR-NOR realization is equivalent to:
  • (a) OR-NOT
  • (b) OR-AND
  • (c) AND-OR
  • (d) NOT-AND
  • (e) b & d
Answer: (b) OR-AND. Two levels of NOR gates implement a product of sums, the same as OR gates followed by an AND gate (Lecture 6, slide 53).
2023 Q30Which of the following is not a degenerate form?
  • (a) NOR-OR
  • (b) NOR-AND
  • (c) AND-NAND
  • (d) NAND-NOR
  • (e) OR-OR
Answer: (a) NOR-OR. Degenerate forms reduce to one operation: AND–AND, AND–NAND, OR–OR, OR–NOR, NAND–OR, NAND–NOR, NOR–AND, NOR–NAND. NOR–OR is the OR-AND-INVERT form and is not degenerate.

Formula sheet for revision

Everything above in one place, with the doctor's steps and the traps. Revise from it once you can do the skills; it is hard to learn from it the first time.

Rules to memorise
  • Column and row order is 00, 01, 11, 10 (Gray code), never 00, 01, 10, 11.
  • Groups hold 1, 2, 4, 8 or 16 squares (a power of 2) and must be rectangles. Edges wrap around; the four corners of a 4-variable map form one group.
  • In an \(n\)-variable map a group of \(2^{\,k}\) squares gives a term of \(n-k\) literals. Four variables: 1 square → 4 literals, 2 → 3, 4 → 2, 8 → 1, 16 → the function is 1.
  • A variable stays in the term if it has the same value in every square of the group: value 1 → unprimed, value 0 → primed.
  • Implicant: any group of 1s. Prime implicant: a group that cannot be made bigger. Essential prime implicant: a prime implicant that covers some 1 that no other prime implicant covers.
  • Procedure: take all essential prime implicants first, then cover the 1s that remain with as few prime implicants as possible. The answer may not be unique.
  • Product of sums: group the 0s to get \(F'\) as a sum of products, then apply DeMorgan: \(F=(F')'\).
  • Don't-care squares (X) may be counted as 1 or as 0, whichever gives bigger groups. They never have to be covered.
  • NAND–NAND implementation: simplify to SOP, one NAND gate per product term, one NAND gate at the output; a term with a single literal goes into the output gate complemented.
  • NOR–NOR implementation: simplify to POS, one NOR per sum term, one NOR at the output.
  • AND–OR and NAND–NAND = sum of products. OR–AND and NOR–NOR = product of sums.
  • AND-OR-INVERT: AND–NOR = NAND–AND, \(F=(AB+CD+E)'\). OR-AND-INVERT: OR–NAND = NOR–OR, \(F=[(A+B)(C+D)E]'\).
  • Of the 16 two-level combinations, 8 are degenerate (they reduce to a single operation): AND–AND, AND–NAND, OR–OR, OR–NOR, NAND–OR, NAND–NOR, NOR–AND, NOR–NAND.
  • The K-map is practical when the number of variables is less than 7 (slide 3).
The doctor's steps for a K-map question
  1. Draw the map with the variable names and the 00, 01, 11, 10 labels.
  2. Write a 1 in each minterm square (and X in each don't-care square).
  3. Circle the largest possible groups; start with the 1s that can be grouped in only one way.
  4. Write one product term per group and OR the terms.
  5. For POS: circle the 0s, write \(F'\), then complement it.
Traps
  • A group of 3 or 6 squares is not allowed.
  • Forgetting the wrap-around: in a 3-variable map, columns 00 and 10 are adjacent (\(m_0\) with \(m_2\), \(m_4\) with \(m_6\)).
  • Adding a group that covers only 1s that are already covered. It adds a redundant term (the red terms on slide 32).
  • Slide 38 writes “\(F'(x,y,z)=\Pi(0,2,5,7)\)” and calls it “sum-of-maxterm”. For Table 3.2 it is \(F=\Pi(0,2,5,7)\), the product of maxterms; equivalently \(F'=\Sigma(0,2,5,7)\).
  • Slide 6 defines minterm adjacency with \(m_5+m_7=xy'z+xyz=xz\). The slide's text is cut at the bottom; this is the complete line.

Sheet 1 (Chapter 1) · problems 1.7 to 1.16

1.7Sheet 1

Convert the hexadecimal number 68BE to binary, and then convert it from binary to octal.

Answer: \((68BE)_{16}=(0110100010111110)_2=(64276)_8\)

Solution on paper
each digit -> 4 bits:
   6    8    B    E  
  0110 1000 1011 1110
groups of 3 bits (zeros added at the ends):
  000 110 100 010 111 110
   0   6   4   2   7   6

Handwritten solution: it converts \((64CD)_{16}\) instead and gets \((0110\,0100\,1100\,1101)_2=(62315)_8\), which is correct for that number:

each digit -> 4 bits:
   6    4    C    D  
  0110 0100 1100 1101
groups of 3 bits (zeros added at the ends):
  000 110 010 011 001 101
   0   6   2   3   1   5
1.8Sheet 1

Convert the decimal number 431 to binary in two ways: (a) directly; (b) first to hexadecimal, then from hexadecimal to binary. Which method is faster?

Answer: \((431)_{10}=(110101111)_2\); method (b) is faster

Solution on paper

(a) Divide by 2:

DivideQuotientRemainderCoefficient
431 ÷ 22151\(a_{0}=\mathrm{1}\) ← LSB
215 ÷ 21071\(a_{1}=\mathrm{1}\)
107 ÷ 2531\(a_{2}=\mathrm{1}\)
53 ÷ 2261\(a_{3}=\mathrm{1}\)
26 ÷ 2130\(a_{4}=\mathrm{0}\)
13 ÷ 261\(a_{5}=\mathrm{1}\)
6 ÷ 230\(a_{6}=\mathrm{0}\)
3 ÷ 211\(a_{7}=\mathrm{1}\)
1 ÷ 201\(a_{8}=\mathrm{1}\) ← MSB

(b) Divide by 16, then write each hex digit as 4 bits:

DivideQuotientRemainderCoefficient
431 ÷ 1626F (=15)\(a_{0}=\mathrm{F}\) ← LSB
26 ÷ 161A (=10)\(a_{1}=\mathrm{A}\)
1 ÷ 1601\(a_{2}=\mathrm{1}\) ← MSB
each digit -> 4 bits:
   1    A    F  
  0001 1010 1111

Method (b) needs 3 divisions instead of 9, so it is faster. The handwritten solution agrees.

1.9Sheet 1

Express the following numbers in decimal: (a) \((10110.0101)_2\) (b) \((16.5)_{16}\) (c) \((26.24)_8\) (d) \((FAFA)_{16}\) (e) \((1010.1010)_2\)

Answer: (a) 22.3125 (b) 22.3125 (c) 22.3125 (d) 64250 (e) 10.625

Solution on paper

(a)

\[\begin{aligned}(\mathrm{10110.0101})_{2} &= 1\times 2^{4} + 0\times 2^{3} + 1\times 2^{2} + 1\times 2^{1} + 0\times 2^{0} + 0\times 2^{-1} + 1\times 2^{-2} + 0\times 2^{-3} + 1\times 2^{-4} \\[3pt] &= 16.0 + 0.0 + 4.0 + 2.0 + 0.0 + 0.0 + 0.25 + 0.0 + 0.0625 \\[3pt] &= \boxed{(\mathrm{22.3125})_{10}}\end{aligned}\]

(b)

\[\begin{aligned}(\mathrm{16.5})_{16} &= 1\times 16^{1} + 6\times 16^{0} + 5\times 16^{-1} \\[3pt] &= 16.0 + 6.0 + 0.3125 \\[3pt] &= \boxed{(\mathrm{22.3125})_{10}}\end{aligned}\]

(c)

\[\begin{aligned}(\mathrm{26.24})_{8} &= 2\times 8^{1} + 6\times 8^{0} + 2\times 8^{-1} + 4\times 8^{-2} \\[3pt] &= 16.0 + 6.0 + 0.25 + 0.0625 \\[3pt] &= \boxed{(\mathrm{22.3125})_{10}}\end{aligned}\]

(d)

\[\begin{aligned}(\mathrm{FAFA})_{16} &= 15\times 16^{3} + 10\times 16^{2} + 15\times 16^{1} + 10\times 16^{0} \\[3pt] &= 61440.0 + 2560.0 + 240.0 + 10.0 \\[3pt] &= \boxed{(\mathrm{64250.0})_{10}}\end{aligned}\]

(e)

\[\begin{aligned}(\mathrm{1010.1010})_{2} &= 1\times 2^{3} + 0\times 2^{2} + 1\times 2^{1} + 0\times 2^{0} + 1\times 2^{-1} + 0\times 2^{-2} + 1\times 2^{-3} + 0\times 2^{-4} \\[3pt] &= 8.0 + 0.0 + 2.0 + 0.0 + 0.5 + 0.0 + 0.125 + 0.0 \\[3pt] &= \boxed{(\mathrm{10.625})_{10}}\end{aligned}\]

(d) in the handwritten solution

\[\begin{aligned}(\mathrm{DADA.B})_{16} &= 13\times 16^{3} + 10\times 16^{2} + 13\times 16^{1} + 10\times 16^{0} + 11\times 16^{-1} \\[3pt] &= 53248.0 + 2560.0 + 208.0 + 10.0 + 0.6875 \\[3pt] &= \boxed{(\mathrm{56026.6875})_{10}}\end{aligned}\]

(e) in the handwritten solution

\[\begin{aligned}(\mathrm{1010.1101})_{2} &= 1\times 2^{3} + 0\times 2^{2} + 1\times 2^{1} + 0\times 2^{0} + 1\times 2^{-1} + 1\times 2^{-2} + 0\times 2^{-3} + 1\times 2^{-4} \\[3pt] &= 8.0 + 0.0 + 2.0 + 0.0 + 0.5 + 0.25 + 0.0 + 0.0625 \\[3pt] &= \boxed{(\mathrm{10.8125})_{10}}\end{aligned}\]

(a), (b) and (c) are the same number written in three bases. The handwritten answers for its own (d) and (e), 56026.6875 and 10.8125, are correct for those numbers.

1.10Sheet 1

Convert the binary numbers (a) 1.10010 and (b) 110.010 to hexadecimal and to decimal. Explain why the decimal answer in (b) is 4 times that in (a).

Answer: (a) \((1.9)_{16}=1.5625\) (b) \((6.4)_{16}=6.25\); the point moved two places to the right, and \(2^2=4\)

Solution on paper
groups of 4 bits (zeros added at the ends):
  0001 . 1001 0000
   1   .  9    0
\[\begin{aligned}(\mathrm{1.10010})_{2} &= 1\times 2^{0} + 1\times 2^{-1} + 0\times 2^{-2} + 0\times 2^{-3} + 1\times 2^{-4} + 0\times 2^{-5} \\[3pt] &= 1.0 + 0.5 + 0.0 + 0.0 + 0.0625 + 0.0 \\[3pt] &= \boxed{(\mathrm{1.5625})_{10}}\end{aligned}\]
groups of 4 bits (zeros added at the ends):
  0110 . 0100
   6   .  4
\[\begin{aligned}(\mathrm{110.010})_{2} &= 1\times 2^{2} + 1\times 2^{1} + 0\times 2^{0} + 0\times 2^{-1} + 1\times 2^{-2} + 0\times 2^{-3} \\[3pt] &= 4.0 + 2.0 + 0.0 + 0.0 + 0.25 + 0.0 \\[3pt] &= \boxed{(\mathrm{6.25})_{10}}\end{aligned}\]

The two numbers have the same digits 110010; in (b) the binary point is two positions further right. Each shift to the right multiplies by 2, so two shifts multiply by 4: \(1.5625\times4=6.25\).

1.11Sheet 1

Perform the following division in binary: 111011 ÷ 101.

Answer: quotient 1011, remainder 100 (59 ÷ 5 = 11 remainder 4)

Solution on paper
          1 0 1 1        <- quotient
      -------------
1 0 1 ) 1 1 1 0 1 1
        1 0 1            101 goes into 111 once
        -----
          1 0 0          bring down 0 -> 100, smaller than 101 -> quotient bit 0
          1 0 0 1        bring down 1 -> 1001
            1 0 1        1001 - 101 = 100
          -------
            1 0 0 1      bring down 1 -> 1001
              1 0 1
            -------
              1 0 0      <- remainder

Check: \(1011_2\times101_2+100_2=11\times5+4=59=111011_2\). The handwritten solution gives the same quotient and remainder.

1.12Sheet 1

Add and multiply without converting to decimal: (a) binary 1011 and 101; (b) hexadecimal 2E and 34.

1.13 (a)Sheet 1

Convert \((\mathrm{27.315})_{10}\) to binary.

Number\((\mathrm{27.315})_{10}\)
From base10
To base2

Find: \((?)_{2}\)

Answer: \((\mathrm{11011.01010000…})_{2}\)

Solution on paper

Integer part: divide by 2 again and again; the remainders, read from the last one up, are the digits.

DivideQuotientRemainderCoefficient
27 ÷ 2131\(a_{0}=\mathrm{1}\) ← LSB
13 ÷ 261\(a_{1}=\mathrm{1}\)
6 ÷ 230\(a_{2}=\mathrm{0}\)
3 ÷ 211\(a_{3}=\mathrm{1}\)
1 ÷ 201\(a_{4}=\mathrm{1}\) ← MSB

Fraction part: multiply by 2 again and again; the integer parts, read from the first one down, are the digits.

MultiplyInteger partFraction leftCoefficient
0.315 × 200.63\(a_{-1}=\mathrm{0}\)
0.63 × 210.26\(a_{-2}=\mathrm{1}\)
0.26 × 200.52\(a_{-3}=\mathrm{0}\)
0.52 × 210.04\(a_{-4}=\mathrm{1}\)
0.04 × 200.08\(a_{-5}=\mathrm{0}\)
0.08 × 200.16\(a_{-6}=\mathrm{0}\)
0.16 × 200.32\(a_{-7}=\mathrm{0}\)
0.32 × 200.64\(a_{-8}=\mathrm{0}\)
\[(\mathrm{27.315})_{10} = \boxed{(\mathrm{11011.01010000…})_{2}}\]
1.13 (b) and (c)Sheet 1

(b) Calculate the binary equivalent of 2/3 out to eight places, convert it back to decimal, and say how close it is to 2/3. (c) Convert the binary result into hexadecimal, then to decimal. Is the answer the same?

Answer: (b) \((0.10101010)_2=0.6640625\), error ≈ 0.0026 (c) \((0.AA)_{16}=0.6640625\), the same

Solution on paper
MultiplyInteger partFraction leftCoefficient
2/3 × 211/3\(a_{-1}=\mathrm{1}\)
1/3 × 202/3\(a_{-2}=\mathrm{0}\)
2/3 × 211/3\(a_{-3}=\mathrm{1}\)
1/3 × 202/3\(a_{-4}=\mathrm{0}\)
2/3 × 211/3\(a_{-5}=\mathrm{1}\)
1/3 × 202/3\(a_{-6}=\mathrm{0}\)
2/3 × 211/3\(a_{-7}=\mathrm{1}\)
1/3 × 202/3\(a_{-8}=\mathrm{0}\)
\[\begin{aligned}(\mathrm{0.10101010})_{2} &= 0\times 2^{0} + 1\times 2^{-1} + 0\times 2^{-2} + 1\times 2^{-3} + 0\times 2^{-4} + 1\times 2^{-5} + 0\times 2^{-6} + 1\times 2^{-7} + 0\times 2^{-8} \\[3pt] &= 0.0 + 0.5 + 0.0 + 0.125 + 0.0 + 0.03125 + 0.0 + 0.0078125 + 0.0 \\[3pt] &= \boxed{(\mathrm{0.6640625})_{10}}\end{aligned}\]

\(2/3=0.6666\ldots\) so the error is \(0.66667-0.66406=0.0026\).

groups of 4 bits (zeros added at the ends):
  0000 . 1010 1010
   0   .  A    A
\[\begin{aligned}(\mathrm{0.AA})_{16} &= 0\times 16^{0} + 10\times 16^{-1} + 10\times 16^{-2} \\[3pt] &= 0.0 + 0.625 + 0.0390625 \\[3pt] &= \boxed{(\mathrm{0.6640625})_{10}}\end{aligned}\]

Same value: hexadecimal is only a shorter way of writing the same bits.
